Baseline score100%without the plugin
Cases33 of 3 ≥ 100% threshold
Perfect runs100%runs where every grader passed
How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nMap the reusable UI contracts a codebase already implements, including patterns hidden in markup, utility classes, CSS, and conventions. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Do not claim complete discovery from keyword matches or recommend abstraction without evidence.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Monorepo with apps and a UI kit. Inventory implicit design-system components, top few consolidation wins, explicit coverage statement. Read-only, no refactor plan. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nMap the reusable UI contracts a codebase already implements, including patterns hidden in markup, utility classes, CSS, and conventions. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Do not claim complete discovery from keyword matches or recommend abstraction without evidence.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c50eaf45-431d-49c8-87a6-854ff88ab31b","uuid":"a5ea8718-b87b-47ff-b392-334e867d4d21","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:29:36.852Z","isSynthetic":true}

Highest value, medium confidence.**\n   - **Why first:** a plain `<div>` with no ARIA role, so nothing tells screen readers it's a status message. Expiry warnings are exactly the kind of message that should be announced. Fixing this once in a component fixes it everywhere.\n   - **Smallest step:** `<Notice tone=\"warning\">`, rendering `role=\"status\"` (or `alert` if it's urgent) with the existing classes. Don't add other tones until a screen needs one.\n2. **Primary button. Medium confidence.**\n   - **Why:** both buttons have no `type` attribute, and neither page shows hover, focus, disabled or loading styles, so every new screen will have to invent its own.\n   - **Smallest step:** `<Button variant=\"primary\">` that defaults `type=\"button\"` and has a visible focus style. Moving the hard-coded colour into a CSS variable (`--color-primary`) costs almost nothing.\n3. **Panel. Low priority.**\n   - **Why low:** it's a pure visual surface with no behaviour. The `.panel` class already does the job.\n   - **Smallest step:** keep it as a documented CSS recipe. Only make it a `<Panel title>` component if the heading-plus-content structure keeps showing up.\n\n**Leave alone:**\n- **Page shell:** two instances isn't enough to justify a layout component yet.\n- **Invoice preview:** domain-specific, and the README says it's intentionally one-off.\n\n**Optional:** move the four hard-coded values (primary blue, amber, border grey, radius) into CSS custom properties. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nMap the reusable UI contracts a codebase already implements, including patterns hidden in markup, utility classes, CSS, and conventions. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Do not claim complete discovery from keyword matches or recommend abstraction without evidence.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"4dd9b7c9-1eee-4404-8d00-1100e427b233","uuid":"77bd2d65-c25e-4618-8b60-dfd3640ce2fe","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:30:05.797Z","isSynthetic":true}

Always a sibling after the panel. | `styles.css:3`; `billing.tsx:1` (\"Card expires soon\"); `team.tsx:1` (\"One invite is expiring\") | **Confirmed.** Used twice, identically |\n| Page layout (`main` > `h1` > panel > notice) | Same order on both pages | `billing.tsx:1`, `team.tsx:1` | **Candidate only.** Two pages isn't enough to call it a layout primitive |\n| `.billing-invoice-preview` | Monospace font | `styles.css:4` only | **Domain-specific and intentionally one-off** (per the README). **It isn't used in either page**, so it's dead CSS or belongs to a screen that isn't in this repo |\n\nSome smaller details are worth knowing:\n- The class names are built to have variants: `.action` + `.primary`, `.notice` + `.warning`. But no base rules exist for `.action` or `.notice` alone, and no other variants exist. The naming suggests a variant system that hasn't been built.\n- The buttons have no `type` attribute, and the notices have no `role` (`status` or `alert`). Each page repeats these accessibility gaps independently.\n\n## Priorities\n1. **Warning notice: make it a small component (high confidence).** It's the only pattern where behaviour matters as well as looks. Screen readers should announce the message, so it needs a `role=\"status\"` or `alert` decision. Making it a component means that decision is made once. A minimal API would be `<Notice tone=\"warning\">…</Notice>`, with `tone` limited to `warning` until a second tone is actually needed.\n2. **Primary action: make it a `Button` with an explicit variant (medium-high confidence).** It's repeated and it's interactive. A wrapper could default `type=\"button\"` and own the focus style, which is currently missing (there's no `:focus-visible` rule). Keep it to `variant=\"primary\"` for now and don't add secondary or ghost variants speculatively.\n3. **Pull the repeated literals into a few CSS custom properties (medium confidence).** These are the primary blue, warning amber and background, border grey, and the radii (12px and 999px). It's cheap, causes no markup churn, and stops one-off hex values spreading as screens are added.\n4. **Panel: keep it as a CSS class for now (it's lower priority).** It's purely visual and already consistent. A `<Panel title>` component might later be worth it so it renders a `<section>` with a labelled heading, but two identical uses don't justify it on their own.\n\n**Leave these as they are:** the invoice preview (domain-specific; first check whether the class is still needed) and the page layout (not enough evidence yet).\n\n## Coverage\n- **Inspected:** all four files in the repo: `README.md`, `src/styles.css`, `src/pages/billing.tsx`, `src/pages/team.tsx`. Within this repo, coverage is exhaustive.\n- **Couldn't check:** git status, because the sandbox blocked running `git`. I couldn't confirm whether the worktree has uncommitted changes.\n- **Not done:** this is a source review only. I didn't render the pages, so nothing here is visually or runtime verified. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nMap the reusable UI contracts a codebase already implements, including patterns hidden in markup, utility classes, CSS, and conventions. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Do not claim complete discovery from keyword matches or recommend abstraction without evidence.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"86742c65-8491-4537-8b10-c8dccd63cd09","uuid":"f5d47085-4a6a-4598-8c76-73440b671988","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:30:36.055Z","isSynthetic":true}

o routes.\n\n### Things I noticed along the way\n\n- **The classes are built as base + variant, but the base doesn't exist.** `.action` and `.notice` have no styles of their own; only the combined `.action.primary` and `.notice.warning` do. Someone was clearly planning more variants (secondary, error, info) that haven't been built. A plain `.action` button would currently get no styling at all.\n- **The button has no interactive states.** There's no hover, keyboard-focus, active or disabled styling, and no explicit `type`. Today it falls back to browser defaults.\n- **Screen readers aren't told about the warnings.** The notice is a plain `<div>` with no `role=\"status\"` or `role=\"alert\"`, so a warning that appears or changes isn't announced. The words themselves make the meaning clear, so it doesn't rely on colour alone.\n- **The invoice preview style looks unused.** The README calls it \"intentionally one-off\", but `billing.tsx` never uses `billing-invoice-preview`. Either the markup lives somewhere I didn't see, or the style is dead.\n\n## What's worth standardizing, in order\n\n1. **Pull the hard-coded values into shared CSS variables** (high confidence). Define the colours, corner radii and spacing once, e.g. `--color-primary`, `--color-warning`, `--color-warning-bg`, `--color-border`, `--radius-card`, `--radius-pill`, `--space-*`. This has the widest effect for the least effort and changes nothing on screen. The next step is a `:root { … }` block in `styles.css`.\n2. **Make the button a real, complete style** (high confidence). Give `.action` its own base style and add hover, `:focus-visible` and disabled states. Hold off on new variants until a screen actually needs one. Of everything here, buttons carry the most behaviour, so they matter most for accessibility. A small `<Button variant=\"primary\">` component is justified only if the states start being copied around; with two uses, a well-made CSS recipe is enough.\n3. **Notice: add a base style and announce it to screen readers** (medium confidence). Give `.notice` a base style so it's ready for more types later, and settle the `role` question. A tiny `<Notice tone=\"warning\">` component would be the natural place to apply the role consistently, and is the one component I'd consider early.\n4. **Panel: keep it as a CSS class** (medium confidence). It's purely visual and works fine as a class. It doesn't need a component unless a standard heading/actions layout appears inside it.\n\n**Leave these local:**\n- **The shared page layout.** Two identical pages isn't enough to say it's a real template. Wait for a third page before extracting it.\n- **The invoice preview.** It's specific to billing, so it should stay out of any shared kit. Check whether it's actually used before keeping it.\n\n## What I covered\n\n- **Read in full:** `README.md`, `src/styles.css`, `src/pages/billing.tsx`, `src/pages/team.tsx`. These are every file in the working tree, so the review covers the whole repo as it exists here.\n- **Git:** the sandbox blocked `git` itself (permission denied), so I couldn't check the git history or whether there were uncommitted changes.\n- **Not checked:** this was a read-through of the source only. I didn't run the app, look at it on screen or measure colour contrast. I didn't change anything. The repo is small, so this covers everything in it.\n\n## The unofficial UI kit\n\nThere's no components folder. The kit is three global CSS classes in `src/styles.css`, used the same way on both pages. Each page has the same layout: `main > h1`, then a panel with an `h2` and a primary button, then a warning notice.\n\n### 1. Panel (card container)\n```css\n.panel { border: 1px solid #ddd; border-radius: 12px; padding: 24px; background: white; }\n```\n| Screen | Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `billing.tsx:1` | `<div className=\"panel\"><h2>Payment method</h2>…</div>` |\n| `team.tsx:1` | `<div className=\"panel\"><h2>Members</h2>…</div>` |\n\nBoth uses look the same: a section heading (`h2`) followed by content. That makes the panel a titled section container in practice, not a plain box.\n\n### 2. Primary action button\n```css\n.action.primary { border-radius: 999px; padding: 10px 18px; background: #1769aa; color: white; }\n```\n| Screen | Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `billing.tsx:1` | `<button className=\"action primary\">Update card</button>` |\n| `team.tsx:1` | `<button className=\"action primary\">Invite member</button>` |\n\nThe classes are written as `.action` plus a `.primary` modifier, which suggests other variants were planned. But `.action` has no styles of its own, and only `primary` exists.\n\n### 3. Warning notice\n```css\n.notice.warning { border-left: 4px solid #d97706; padding: 12px; background: #fff4d6; }\n```\n| Screen | Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `billing.tsx:1` | `<div className=\"notice warning\">Card expires soon</div>` |\n| `team.tsx:1` | `<div className=\"notice warning\">One invite is expiring</div>` |\n\nSame base-plus-modifier setup: `warning` is the only variant, and `.notice` alone is unstyled. Both messages are about something expiring.\n\n### Not part of the kit\n- **`.billing-invoice-preview`** (`font-family: monospace`): the README says this one is deliberately one-off, and it's named after one screen. Leave it alone. It's also not used in `billing.tsx` right now, so it's either dead CSS or waiting for a preview that hasn't been built yet.\n- **The page frame** (`<main><h1>…</h1>…</main>`) repeats too, but it has no styling and is just plain HTML. It's too thin to turn into a component yet.\n\n## What's worth standardizing\n\nAll three patterns are worth it, but keep each one minimal.\n\n1. **Wrap the three patterns as small React components.** `Panel`, `Button` and `Notice` should be thin wrappers around the existing classes. The point is to stop people typing class strings by hand, where a typo like `\"action primay\"` fails silently.\n   - `Panel` could take a `title` prop, since both uses start with an `h2`.\n   - `Button` and `Notice` should start with only the variant that exists (`primary`, `warning`). Add a `secondary` or `info` variant when a screen actually needs one, not before.\n\n2. **Move the repeated colors into CSS variables.** `#1769aa`, `#d97706`, `#fff4d6`, `#ddd` and `white` are hardcoded. They're each used once today, so the payoff is small. It becomes worthwhile when you add a second variant or dark mode. Same for the spacing values (24/18/12/10px): don't build a spacing scale until more screens exist to show what the scale should be.\n\n3. **Document what exists.** Three short lines in the README (\"use Panel for titled sections…\") would make the kit official at almost no cost.\n\n**What I'd skip for now:** a separate shared-components package, Storybook, a full token system, or an `<Alert>` with every severity level. With two screens and three patterns, that would be building ahead of real need. A single `src/components/` folder is enough. Revisit once a third or fourth screen shows whether these patterns hold up or need to change."}],"container":null,"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":594,"cache_read_input_tokens":16389,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":594},"output_tokens":3,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"global","speed":"standard"},"input_transformations":[],"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"19243274-b632-4e89-91c8-a37a91ec6cfd","uuid":"26b46ebe-6af6-4bb6-8447-26be1a6faa3f","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:31:27.146Z","request_id":"req_011Cgww5roMsAJwcGMBcqNma"}

Warning notice (a callout with an amber left border)**: `src/styles.css:3`\n```css\n.notice.warning { border-left: 4px solid #d97706; padding: 12px; background: #fff4d6; }\n```\n- Billing: `<div className=\"notice warning\">Card expires soon</div>`\n- Team: `<div className=\"notice warning\">One invite is expiring</div>`\n\nThis uses the same `base + variant` naming as the button, and again `warning` is the only variant.\n\n**Page layout:** both pages also follow the same structure: `<main>` → `<h1>` → panel → notice. That's a shared layout, though it isn't a component yet.\n\n## Not a pattern\n`.billing-invoice-preview` (`src/styles.css:4`) is a one-off, which the README also says. Neither page uses it, so either it's dead CSS or it belongs to a screen that isn't in this repo. It shouldn't go into a shared kit.\n\n## What's worth standardizing\n\n1. **Turn the three patterns into components (`Panel`, `Button`, `Notice`)**, which together cover all the reused UI. A `Panel` with a `title` prop would also fix the `<div>` + `<h2>` pairing in one place. Keep the existing class names so the change is just a wrapper.\n2. **Make the hard-coded values into tokens**: colors (`#1769aa`, `#d97706`, `#fff4d6`, `#ddd`), radii (`12px`, `999px`) and spacing (`24px`, `18px`, `12px`, `10px`). Even a small set of CSS custom properties would stop these values drifting apart as more screens are added.\n3. **Keep variant names but add variants only when a screen needs one.** The `primary`/`warning` naming is a good convention, but don't add secondary, danger, info and so on before anything uses them.\n4. **Fix a missing piece while you're there:** the warning notice has no role, so screen readers won't announce it. A shared `Notice` component is the natural place to add `role=\"status\"` or `role=\"alert\"` once for every page.\n\n**Not worth doing yet:** a separate shared-components package, a page-layout component, or documentation for the system. With two screens and three patterns, a `src/components/` folder plus a tokens block at the top of `styles.css` is enough. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nMap the reusable UI contracts a codebase already implements, including patterns hidden in markup, utility classes, CSS, and conventions. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Do not claim complete discovery from keyword matches or recommend abstraction without evidence.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"9481c2c0-e4b3-467a-a03c-2978cd0b774f","uuid":"413c6a1d-8305-43a2-adb0-10b74ce87d39","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:32:19.836Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nMap the reusable UI contracts a codebase already implements, including patterns hidden in markup, utility classes, CSS, and conventions. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Do not claim complete discovery from keyword matches or recommend abstraction without evidence.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Audit admin screens for implicit design-system components; shared UI package exists. Read-only. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nMap the reusable UI contracts a codebase already implements, including patterns hidden in markup, utility classes, CSS, and conventions. Produce evidence and restrained recommendations—not a component wishlist or redesign.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Inspect repository instructions and `git status`; preserve existing work. Read the design docs, token sources, shared-component API, exports/catalog, and drift checks before surveying screens. Treat implementation as evidence and docs as intent.\n2. Map the explicit primitives and their ownership. Search app routes, local components, and styles for repeated structure, appearance, states, and behavior. Compare candidate instances; class-name similarity alone is not proof. Use UI families (actions, fields, surfaces, feedback, overlays, data display, navigation, identity, loading/empty/error) as search leads, not a checklist.\n3. Separate shared primitives, implicit local twins, genuinely missing shared patterns, and domain-specific compositions. A one-off or domain component is not automatically a design-system primitive. Note documentation/code drift and label uncertain inferences.\n4. Prioritize only a few opportunities by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, accessibility/behavior risk, and blast radius. Recommend the smallest useful shared API or recipe; explain when a pattern should remain local.\n5. Report exactly which apps/packages/routes or representative files you inspected, what was excluded, and whether coverage is scoped or exhaustive. A search is not proof of completeness.\n\n## Report\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n- **Summary:** existing explicit foundation and main finding.\n- **Explicit foundation:** authoritative docs, tokens, primitives, catalogs, and enforcement.\n- **Implicit inventory:** table of pattern, repeated contract/variants, concrete source paths, and relation to shared primitives. Group examples; distinguish confirmed repetition from candidates needing review.\n- **Priorities:** ranked, concise consolidation opportunities with evidence, rationale, smallest next step, and confidence.\n- **Coverage:** inspected surfaces, exclusions, dirty-worktree caveats, and limits. Do not imply visual/runtime verification from source review alone.\n\nStay read-only unless implementation is explicitly requested. Do not claim complete discovery from keyword matches or recommend abstraction without evidence.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"4b9d6176-57fd-45cf-bc84-5ed046dd33c9","uuid":"1378f5f7-6521-4591-8c46-283061c068eb","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:33:27.918Z","isSynthetic":true}

These are just a hand-built `<Button variant=\"primary\">`.\n\n**3. A section header pattern: 2 copies**\nBoth routes open with a panel that contains an `<h2>` and one action button. That shape could become a `PageHeader` or `Card` header slot. With only two examples, though, I'd treat it as a candidate rather than a confirmed component.\n\n**4. An empty state: 1 copy**\n`users.tsx:1` renders \"No users yet\" as a bare card. There's only one instance and no `EmptyState` export, so this isn't worth extracting yet. Watch for it in other routes.\n\n## What should stay domain-specific\n\n- **`audit.tsx:1` `<table className=\"audit-grid\">`**: `docs/design.md:2` explicitly allows dense data tables to stay local \"when behavior differs\". The package does export `DataTable`, but its source isn't in the repo, so I can't tell whether the audit grid behaves differently. My default is to leave it local and check `DataTable` before deciding.\n- **Workflow-specific status panels**: the design doc allows these to stay local too. None exist in these routes yet.\n\n## What I'd consolidate first\n\n1. **Buttons → `Button`.** The design doc rule is clearest here and the copies are identical. There's a problem, though: `packages/ui/src/Button.tsx:1` only accepts `variant` and `children`. It doesn't pass through `onClick`, `type`, `disabled`, `className` or `...rest`. A real Invite or Export button needs at least `onClick`, so the shared `Button` probably has to accept and forward native button props before the admin screens can use it. As written, a straight swap would lose functionality as soon as these buttons get handlers.\n2. **Panels → `Card`.** There are more copies (4), and once they use `Card`, the 12px radius comes from tokens instead of being hardcoded. I put this second only because I couldn't see `Card`'s API to confirm it can render as a `<section>` and fit a header.\n\n## Coverage, honestly\n\n- **Read:** every file in the repo.
